#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""全篇数字溯源审计：把正文里的数字抽出来，查它是否出现在任何数据文件/原始日志里。

⚠ 本脚本的能力边界（2026-09-24 记）：
  它只把正文数字与 paper/data/*.csv 做匹配，【不理解派生关系】。所以
  「找不到」只是提示"这个数不在 CSV 里"，不等于"它没有出处" —— 项目纪律允许的
  出处是「代码、日志或 paper/data/*.csv」，从 results/ 原始日志当场算出来的
  派生量（比率、区间、极差）本来就不在 CSV 里。
  真要把它们也消掉，办法是给每一组派生量建生成器（本目录下已经有若干个：
  reuse / reuse_x / hitrate / toolwait / idle_pooled / table1 …），不是放宽本脚本。
  2026-09-24 从 85 处降到 30 余处，绝大多数是【建生成器】消掉的，
  过程中还查出本脚本自己两个 bug：千分位花括号匹配不了、负数不取绝对值。

为什么需要：这个项目反复栽在"数字追不到源头"上 —— 手抄表、从错的列抄数、
用了子集却没写下来。审稿人每一轮都能抓到几个。与其等人抓，不如自己先扫一遍。

用法: python3 audit_numbers.py            # 列出所有"可疑"数字（数据文件里找不到的）
      python3 audit_numbers.py --all      # 连找到的一起列，附出处
"""
import glob, os, re, sys

ROOT = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)), "..", "..")
TEX = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(ROOT, "paper", "*.tex")))
DATA = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(ROOT, "paper", "data", "*.csv")))

# 正文里可能是"测量值"的数字：至少两位有效数字、不是年份/页码/引用号
# 千分位有两种写法：LaTeX 的 1{,}849 与纯 CSV 的 1,849。原模式里的
# [,{][,}]?\d{3} 匹配不了 {,} —— `{` 后面是 `,` 不是数字，于是 1{,}849 只被
# 匹配到开头的 1，剩下的 849 又被当成一个独立数字报成"找不到源头"，
# 一口气制造 16 处假阳性（2026-09-24 查出来的）。改成显式二选一。
# 另补一条 \d{4,}：不加它的话【裸写】的大整数（如 1849）反而匹配不上 ——
# 前一条要求有千分位分隔符或小数点。
NUM = re.compile(r"(?<![\w.])(\d{1,3}(?:(?:\{,\}|,)\d{3})*(?:\.\d+)?|\d{4,}|\d+\.\d+)(?![\w])")

def norm(tok):
    return tok.replace("{,}", "").replace(",", "").replace("\\,", "")

def load_data_numbers():
    """数据文件里出现过的所有数字（字符串形式，含各种舍入）。

    注意：**必须按 CSV 字段解析，不能用正则扫整行** —— 正则里的千分位逗号
    逻辑会把列分隔符也吃掉（"200,999,260" 被当成一个数），于是行内的真值
    反而进不了集合，审计会满屏假阳性。这个 bug 犯过一次。
    """
    import csv as _csv
    vals = set()

    def add(x):
        # d 必须到 4：CSV 里有 4 位小数的列（reuse.csv 的 reuse_frac/realized_frac
        # 就是 %.4f），而 d 只到 3 时 round(0.6917,3)=0.692 ≠ 0.6917，于是这些
        # 明明在数据文件里的值被报成"找不到"——2026-09-24 发现，当时满屏假阳性。
        for d in (0, 1, 2, 3, 4):
            for y in (x, -x, abs(x)):
                # 绝对值也要加：正文里的 Δ 全是负的，写成 $-7.8$，正则提取出的是
                # 正的 7.8，而数据列里是 -7.8 —— 不加 abs 会让整列 Δ 都被报成
                # "找不到源头"（2026-09-24 审计报了十几个）。
                vals.add(round(y, d))
                vals.add(round(y / 100.0, d))     # 百分比 vs 小数
                vals.add(round(y * 100.0, d))

    for p in DATA:
        with open(p, newline="") as f:
            for row in _csv.reader(l for l in f if not l.startswith("#")):
                for cell in row:
                    cell = cell.strip()
                    if not cell:
                        continue
                    try:
                        add(float(cell))
                        continue
                    except ValueError:
                        pass
                    # 单元格里可能嵌着数字（如 "1.42 to 1.30"）
                    for m in NUM.finditer(cell.replace(",", "")):
                        try:
                            add(float(m.group(1)))
                        except ValueError:
                            pass
    return vals

def main():
    have = load_data_numbers()
    show_all = "--all" in sys.argv
    suspicious = []
    for p in TEX:
        # 去掉注释行和 preamble
        for i, line in enumerate(open(p), 1):
            if line.lstrip().startswith("%"):
                continue
            for m in NUM.finditer(line):
                tok = norm(m.group(1))
                try:
                    x = float(tok)
                except ValueError:
                    continue
                # 明显不是测量值的：年份、小节号、页数、常见常量
                if x in (2.0, 10.0, 24.0, 200.0, 3090.0, 2026.0, 2025.0, 2024.0):
                    continue
                if abs(x - round(x)) < 1e-9 and 1 <= x <= 12:
                    continue          # 小节号/枚举
                if x < 0.05:
                    continue          # 概率/比率一类，多半在表里
                hit = any(abs(x - h) < 1e-6 for h in have)
                if not hit:
                    suspicious.append((os.path.basename(p), i, m.group(1), line.strip()[:96]))
                elif show_all:
                    print("OK   %-18s:%-4d %-10s" % (os.path.basename(p), i, m.group(1)))
    print("\n=== 数据文件里找不到的数字：%d 处 ===" % len(suspicious))
    for f, i, tok, ctx in suspicious:
        print("%-18s:%-4d  %-10s  %s" % (f, i, tok, ctx))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

for cap3$\to$cap4. Only the second band crosses one. So the sign of

\paragraph{The controller's verdict transfers, weakly.} \sys{} finishes the
second trace in 75.6 minutes; the fastest static \emph{run} on this trace
finishes in 66.4 and the fastest of the three caps that build its envelope in
66.9, so \sys{} is 13.9\% slower than the first and 13.2\% than the second
(13.89\% and 13.15\% at full precision)---but that gap is inside the run-to-run
spread we measure elsewhere (up to 22.4\% between repeat pairs of the same
configuration), so we do not claim it lost. What we can say is that it did not win, on either axis:
its signed distance from the second trace's static envelope is $+0.40$\,pp on
the SLO axis---inside the $\pm1.0$\,pp tie band, which is defined on that
axis---and $-0.14$\,pp on the cache-hit axis, against the 6.3 points that
separate this trace's own static caps on that axis.
The SLO number is nearly uninformative, and we flag that rather than let it
stand as a measurement: at a 10\,s threshold every point on this trace scores
99.5--100.0\%, so the axis cannot discriminate: the whole spread between its
fastest and slowest configuration is half a point, inside the tie band
configuration where \S\ref{sec:model} says added concurrency is expensive. The two workloads differ in ways that matter for
this paper, which is why we chose it. Its sessions are smaller (median prompt
18{,}809 tokens against 23{,}187) and its tool waits are far longer at the
median: over round transitions---same session,
adjacent rounds, each session's last round excluded because it has no
successor---the gap is 20.2\,s against 0.34\,s at the median---about 60$\times$---and
39.9\,s against 15.8\,s at the mean, which is 2.5$\times$
(\texttt{toolwait.csv} carries both traces on this definition). The 29.1\,s
that appears in \texttt{elasticity\_x.csv} is the mean over all 390 rows
including each session's last round, and is not comparable with the main
trace's 15.8\,s; we use the transition definition throughout §\ref{sec:xtrace}. So the engine is idle far more of the
time, and the running counts are lower.

We run the same controller and the same cap family on a 25-session tier. The
throttle configuration is reversed: with all safety throttles disabled, the
25-session tier runs \emph{51\% faster} (58.4 to 28.8 minutes) at a cost of
only 2.4\,pp of SLO, while on the 200-session tier disabling the same
throttles costs 42\% (291.3 to 415.0 minutes) and 43.7\,pp of SLO. One switch,
two regimes, opposite signs, both around 40--50\%
(Fig.~\ref{fig:loadtier}). No single scalar setting is optimal across both.

D=collections.defaultdict(list)

        stated hypothesis, because they were not.
  \item We give a two-part decomposition of the wall clock (\S\ref{sec:model})
        that locates the cancellation: admission policy moves the
        running-request count, and the per-step cost moves with it. On the
        device-only configurations we can account for that rise token for
        token---evicted prefixes are recomputed almost one for one. On
        host-tier configurations recomputation is flat and the account has to
        come from elsewhere; we report the part of that residual we were able
        to attribute, and the part we were not (\S\ref{sec:model},
        \S\ref{sec:repro}).
  \item We reduce the question to one measurable number, the elasticity of
        per-step cost with respect to the running count (\S\ref{sec:criterion}),
        and measure it at $0.38$ on the model-consistent step cost and $0.61$
        on the per-request one, on the configurations whose mechanism we can
        account for token for token. We offer it as a diagnostic rather than a
        predictor: it says where on the curve a policy operates and how much
        the remaining headroom is worth, and \S\ref{sec:criterion} is explicit
        about the sense in which it is circular and the sense in which it is
        not.
  \item We characterize when adaptivity \emph{could} have paid, and show that
        our workload is not that case: the same throttle configuration that is
        worth $+51\%$ at 25 sessions costs $-42\%$ at 200 (\S\ref{sec:load}).
        Put differently, no single scalar cap is optimal across both, which is
        the regime adaptive control exists for. On the low-load tier, where
        that argument bites hardest, the controller is \emph{not} worse---%
        same-generation measurements put it on the front, $+0.9$\,pp above the
        line and inside the noise. On the high-load tier it is dominated. That
        asymmetry, not a blanket failure, is what this paper measures.
  \item We make the measurements reproducible: the front and each controller's
        signed distance are produced by one script from the raw per-step logs,
        the tables and the sub-thousand-step runs by two others, all named in
        \S\ref{sec:repro}. We report the six claims we had to retract along the
        way, with the checks that caught them.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{What we do not claim}
controller is a different point on the front rather than a dominated one,
which is exactly what the 200-session tier denies it.

We are careful about the size, and about the yardstick. $+0.9$\,pp is
measured against a straight-line interpolation between static points, not
against a switching policy anyone ran, so the defensible statement is that
\sys{} is not dominated by any static point we measured on this tier---not
that it beats what a static mix could achieve. On 125 requests one request is
worth 0.8\,pp, so the margin is about one request. The tier's
segment-to-segment SD (5.1 to 21.4\,pp) is not the right yardstick: segment
volatility is not run-to-run uncertainty. The right one is the replicate spread
we have---which, across the five configurations we repeated, is 0.5, 0.8, 1.0,
3.9 and 5.1\,pp (\texttt{replicates.csv}). Against the two tightest of those $+0.9$\,pp is about a replicate
wide; against the three widest---which belong to the configurations that decide
our headline elsewhere---it is inside one. We quote the tightest pair
because it is the least favourable to us, not because it is the whole
distribution. What we will defend is the weaker claim: on the
low-load tier the controller is not worse, and the same switch that is worth
$+51\%$ there costs $-42\%$ at high load. A controller that is competitive
where load is light and dominated where load is heavy is the pattern this
paper set out to explain, and it is the pattern we measured.

That gives the criterion of \S\ref{sec:criterion} a mechanism rather than a
correlation, and it suggests a prediction we are testing:
if the tier acts by multiplying usable capacity (the host pool is twice the
device pool, so three times in total), then shrinking the device pool alone
should move the collapse point even with no host tier involved. We do not
treat the arithmetic as a result---it assumes KV demand grows linearly in the
running count, which heterogeneous sessions need not satisfy---and the
queued experiment measures the collapse point directly rather than predicting
it, and \S\ref{sec:results} reports it once it has run.

\paragraph{What is left unexplained.} Not everything here is accounted for.
The host-tier runs sit at a narrow band of recomputed tokens (8.6--10.4\,M)
\emph{and} of realization (84.2--92.4\%), yet $\tau_1$ spans 27.0--36.2\,ms
across them.
Part of that is now explained and part is not. Four of those runs
(\texttt{F:hz2} 33.6, \texttt{F:bres} 34.1, \texttt{F:hz0} 35.7,
\texttt{F:breshz1} 36.2\,ms) were
measured while three other engine instances shared the host, which inflates
per-step cost by 25--40\% and leaves the volume columns untouched
\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}

An agentic LLM session is not a request. It is a long-lived job that issues a
round of inference, waits on a tool, and comes back---tens of times---with a
context that grows every round. On a memory-constrained accelerator this makes
admission control look like the obvious lever. The KV pool is the binding
resource; a single large session can occupy most of it; and the sessions
themselves are wildly heterogeneous. If a scheduler can see how much of the
pool a candidate will need and how much room is left, surely it should beat a
fixed number.

We believed this, built a controller that does exactly that, and spent a
considerable amount of GPU time discovering that on our workload and hardware
it is false. This paper reports that negative result, and the negative result is
most of what we have. Alongside it we report one measurement---the elasticity of
per-step cost with respect to the running count---that we hoped would say in
advance how much room admission control has. It does bound that room on the
configurations we measured, but when we tested whether it explains our
controllers' failure, on their own configuration and on a second workload, it
did not. We report it as a diagnostic of a workload, not as a predictor of
whether a controller will win.

\subsection{What we measured}

We serve a replay of 200 real coding-agent sessions on a single RTX 3090
(Qwen2.5-Coder-7B, 96K context, SGLang, a 101{,}432-token KV pool) and compare
a family of admission policies against a sweep of static concurrency caps.
Nine controller variants, spanning four mechanistically distinct ideas, all
land below the static Pareto front, and so does a reimplementation of an
independent congestion-based controller from the literature
(Fig.~\ref{fig:frontier}). The best of them misses by under a point---inside the
$\pm1.0$\,pp band we call a tie, but on the below side of it---and the closest
arm we repeated misses by 0.9 with its repeat at 5.6.

\begin{figure}[tb]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig1_frontier.pdf}
  \caption{Every completed run on the (wall clock, SLO) plane. The grey line
  is the upper-left envelope of the static points---drawn from the data, so it
  uses each configuration's \emph{better} run where a repeat exists, which is

We traced one such window in a repeat of our best configuration and found a
code path that explains it: single-flight admission considered only the head of
the FIFO queue, and when the head was a session too large to fit the remaining
budget it was never admitted, never left the queue, and blocked every smaller
session behind it. That is the code every run in this paper executed. The fix
in our repository---scan the queue, still admit at most one per cycle---is
\emph{not} in the artifact that produced these results, so the numbers here
predate it and we do not claim them for it. We are also explicit that this is
\emph{a} path, not a proven cause:
the audit is a log signature, and the same signature can be produced by other
closed gates. What we can say is that the signature is present in a third of
our \sys{} runs and absent from all of our static caps, which have no
admission decision to get stuck in.

That is the honest version of a finding we first reported too strongly. An
intermediate draft of this paper called it a deadlock and said the
configuration that produced our best result was the one that deadlocked on its
repeat. Neither holds: the windows are ~160\,s and self-clearing, and the best
configuration never showed one. The windows are also too small (0.9\%) to move
any verdict we report. We keep them because the mechanism is real and its size
is the point: on a pool that holds four median sessions, a controller's failure
mode is a silent stall, not graceful degradation, and it took a repeat run to

We note that this claim had already been falsified once by source inspection:
SGLang's \texttt{token\_usage} subtracts evictable blocks, so a parked
session's prefix is genuinely not counted, and charging for it reserves
capacity that really will be needed back. The end-to-end measurement agrees
with the source reading.

\subsection{Route 2: the growth reserve}

Sessions grow by roughly 2.5K tokens per round, so the controller reserves
capacity for the next \texttt{horizon} rounds. We swept the horizon over
$\{0,1,2,3\}$ and the reserve is not a free parameter to be minimised:
Fig.~\ref{fig:horizon} shows the response is non-monotone. Removing the
reserve entirely costs 12.6\,pp of SLO, and a reserve of two rounds is
15 minutes \emph{slower} than three. The optimum is at one round, and there it comes closest to the
static front---0.93\,pp below it, inside the tie band---and that single point is
an admission policy sets. Two configurations with the same mean running count
make this visible: \texttt{horizon}=1 (1.52) and \texttt{cap3}+HiCache (1.51)
idle 36.7 and 24.1 minutes respectively---a 52\% difference at matched
concurrency---and 12.6 of the 19.4-minute wall-clock gap between them is that
idle difference rather than step cost. So the gap durations are the workload's,
and the number of gaps is partly the policy's.

We note one claim we had to drop here. An earlier draft reported that idle
fraction falls monotonically with mean concurrency. With the full data it does
not: on the busy-conditional measure this section uses throughout,
\texttt{F:hz0} at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.67 idles 15.2\% while
\texttt{cap4} at 1.65 idles 10.4\%---more concurrency and \emph{more} idle,
the reverse of the reported trend. (The two arms read 1.41 and 1.48 on the
all-samples mean, where the ordering happens to run the other way;
\texttt{arm\_running.csv} carries both conventions side by side, and we name
the one each claim uses.) The weaker statement that survives is the one we use---%
the stretches are structural and long.

The second thing is that disabling it does not make the controller win.
\texttt{sf=0} finishes in 263.3 minutes at 86.3\% SLO against the base run's
291.3 and 89.8\%: it buys 28 minutes for 3.5\,pp, and its signed distance from
the front moves from $-1.24$ to $-0.55$---onto the tie band from below it, not
above it. Its admission rate is unchanged (2.5\% of cycles admit, against
2.3\%). So the gate was a real cost and removing it moves the arm along the
front rather than past it, which is the same trade every other route in this
section makes.

therefore sets whether added concurrency is paid for in recomputation. The

\paragraph{The second trace agrees on direction and not on level.}
We ran the same accounting on a second workload, reported in full in
\S\ref{sec:xtrace}---117 real Claude agent sessions, 390 rounds, the same pool
and the same engine---and it reproduces the direction: its realization of \emph{its own} bound falls from 74.8\% to 64.0\%
as the running count rises from 1.08 to 1.41, and over that span the whole-range
per-unit slope is $-32.3$ points, against the device-only family's $-66.8$ over
$\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00--1.84 (\texttt{slope.csv}). What it does not reproduce is the level, and this is
worth stating because it bounds what we can claim. The lightest cap on that
trace (	exttt{realhr\_cap2}) runs at a \emph{lower} median device occupancy
than the lightest on this one (15.8\% against 22.4\%, \texttt{cap1}) and
realizes \emph{less} (74.8\% against 92.5\%), so realization is not a function
of occupancy alone---and a
statement of the form ``the pool collapses above $x$\% occupancy'' is wrong on
our own data. Occupancy is instantaneous while reuse depends on history; a
session's prefix must survive from one round to the next, and how much
pressure that puts on the pool is not visible in what the pool holds at an
instant. We do not have the measurement that would separate those, which is
why the controlled experiment we report in \S\ref{sec:results} varies the
pool alone, on one card and one trace, rather than leaning on the cross-trace
comparison. The claim this section supports is the narrow one: within a
configuration, the host tier preserves realization at a matched running count
and a matched occupancy, and without it realization falls.

That gives the criterion of \S\ref{sec:criterion} a mechanism rather than a
correlation, and it suggests a prediction we are testing:
if the tier acts by multiplying usable capacity (the host pool is twice the
device pool, so three times in total), then shrinking the device pool alone
should move the collapse point even with no host tier involved. We do not
treat the arithmetic as a result---it assumes KV demand grows linearly in the
running count, which heterogeneous sessions need not satisfy---and the
queued experiment measures the collapse point directly rather than predicting
it, and \S\ref{sec:results} reports it once it has run.

\paragraph{What is left unexplained.} Not everything here is accounted for.
The host-tier runs sit at a narrow band of recomputed tokens (8.6--10.4\,M)
\emph{and} of realization (84.2--92.4\%), yet $\tau_1$ spans 27.0--36.2\,ms
across them.
Part of that is now explained and part is not. Four of those runs
(\texttt{F:hz2} 33.6, \texttt{F:bres} 34.1, \texttt{F:hz0} 35.7,

falls inside the span its own static points cover (1.077, 1.161, 1.287)---more
precisely inside the cap3$\to$cap4 segment, where
$\varepsilon(\tau_1) = -0.28$ over $\overline{R}$ 1.16--1.29. The other
measured segment, cap2$\to$cap3, is $-2.40$. Both are negative, so nothing in
this trace's measured range says added concurrency was self-defeating, and the
controller's failure is again not explained by the criterion. What this trace
cannot do is test the criterion's \emph{expensive} branch: the highest running
count it reaches---a mean of 1.287 over busy time, with an instantaneous peak
of four in flight---never approaches the $\overline{R} \approx 1.5$ where the
main trace's cliff begins. So the cliff is untested here, neither confirmed nor
refuted, and we do not read this trace's negative result as evidence about it.

We also record what the noise does, because it is the same weakness the whole
frontier at 5\,s and 20\,s as well, from the same per-request logs. The
ordering of the static caps does not change. What changes is where the

\paragraph{What the engine is doing when it is not decoding.} SGLang exposes
two counters that answer this directly: tokens evicted from the radix cache,
and prompt tokens that had to be prefilled rather than reused. Across the five
static caps \emph{without} HiCache the two track each other almost exactly.
\texttt{cap1} evicts 9.2\,M tokens and prefills 8.6\,M uncached;
\texttt{cap5} evicts 21.3\,M and prefills 20.7\,M; the gap is 0.6--0.7\,M on
every one of the five. An evicted prefix is recomputed, close to one token for
one, and that recomputation is chunked into decode steps. Those same five runs
give recomputation rising with $\tau$ across four of the five points: 9.2\,M
uncached tokens at 27.4\,ms, 11.5 at 29.7, 15.4 at 32.4, 20.7 at 36.7. The
exception is the first point, \texttt{cap1}, which recomputes least (8.6\,M)

codex
I found two manuscript discrepancies outside the in-flight items: a later paragraph narrows the host-tier range without naming its subset, and the SF=0 result rounds the admitting-cycle rate to 2.5% where the log gives 2.44% (2.4% to one decimal). I’m finishing the data-file and reused-number audit now.
codex
## (1) The host-tier claim

**MATCH, for the stated 200-session trace after the documented exclusion.** [reuse.csv](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/reuse.csv:19) has 24 rows: five device-only and nineteen host-tier. Recomputing every row’s prompt and uncached-token sums from its `steps.jsonl` gave no CSV mismatches. The host-tier endpoints are **72.8048%** (`foyfix_hc`) and **92.4539%** (`pairA_cap2hc`), which print as 72.8–92.5% ([reuse.csv](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/reuse.csv:38), [reuse.csv](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/reuse.csv:25)).

The metadata search found twenty completed or nearly completed HiCache runs on `replay_pois200_l04.csv`: those nineteen plus the explicitly excluded `foyer_hz1_r2`. None of the other nineteen falls outside the printed range. The search also found six full runs on a **different arrival trace** and several unfinished runs; applying the full-trace bound to an unfinished run would give a misleading percentage.

**MATCH on the exclusion evidence.** `foyer_hz1_r2` has 999 successful steps, 999 distinct request IDs, and a step-log plan of 28,526,871 prompt tokens. Its engine counter ends at 40,095,216—**1.4055×** the plan—while retractions end at zero ([metadata.json](/root/paper/foyer/results/night/pois200_foyer_hz1_r2/metadata.json:4), [steps.jsonl](/root/paper/foyer/results/night/pois200_foyer_hz1_r2/steps.jsonl:1), [metrics.csv](/root/paper/foyer/results/night/pois200_foyer_hz1_r2/metrics.csv:3191)).

**NEW MISMATCH:** A later paragraph says “the host-tier runs” occupy **8.6–10.4 M recomputed tokens** and **84.2–92.4% realization** ([sec_model.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_model.tex:339)). For all nineteen, the ranges are **8.612–12.844 M** and **72.805–92.454%**. The printed narrower range omits at least `foyfix_hc` and `pairA_cap2hc` without naming a subset.

## (2) Table I’s concurrency column

**MATCH.** Independent admission-event and session-span calculations agree to two decimals for every requested check:

| Run | Admission log | Session spans |
|---|---:|---:|
| cap1 | 0.999502 → **1.00** | 0.999494 → **1.00** |
| cap2 | 1.989725 → **1.99** | 1.989715 → **1.99** |
| cap5 | 4.894746 → **4.89** | 4.894731 → **4.89** |
| cap2_25 | 1.953997 → **1.95** | 1.953989 → **1.95** |

The no-control run has no admission events. Its session spans give **94.9083 → 94.9**, matching Table I and [runs.csv](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/runs.csv:15). The estimator definitions are in [gen_runs.py](/root/paper/foyer/scripts/lab/gen_runs.py:54), and the caption states the substitution at [sec_results.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_results.tex:84).

## (3) The SF=0 probe

**MATCH on the newly disclosed two-decimal and composition figures.** Counting the specified controller cycles gives:

| Run | Admitting cycles | Blocked cycles | Remainder of blocked | Over-budget of blocked |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| base `hc` | 206/8,784 = **2.3452%** | 8,196/8,784 = **93.3060%** | 4,491/8,196 = **54.7950%** | 3,148/8,196 = **38.4090%** |
| repeat `hc_r2` | 208/8,830 = **2.3556%** | 8,236/8,830 = **93.2729%** | 4,799/8,236 = **58.2686%** | 2,952/8,236 = **35.8426%** |
| `sf0_r2` | 194/7,945 = **2.4418%** | 7,406/7,945 = **93.2159%** | 2,943/7,406 = **39.7381%** | 3,262/7,406 = **44.0454%** |

Thus 2.35→2.44%, 93.3→93.2%, 54.8→39.7%, 38.4→44.0%, and the repeat’s 58.3% all check out ([gate_attrib.csv](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/gate_attrib.csv:11), [controller.jsonl](/root/paper/foyer/results/night/pois200_foyer_hc/controller.jsonl:1)).

**NEW MISMATCH:** The following paragraph calls the same admission rates “**2.5% … against 2.3%**” ([sec_results.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_results.tex:239)). To one decimal, the computed figures are **2.4% against 2.3%**. The conclusion that the aggregate rate barely moved remains supported.

## (4) Numbers in data files

**MATCH** for the requested additions. [tier25.csv](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/tier25.csv:7) contains all four Table II rows; recomputation through `run_summary.py` matches their printed wall time, concurrency, SLO, segment SD and TTFT. [projection.csv](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/projection.csv:20) records `growth_p50=26762`. [gate_attrib.csv](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/gate_attrib.csv:16) records **51, 206, 3700, 9** in the requested columns. [toolwait.csv](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/toolwait.csv:13) records **1.0007** and **1.0652**.

[**arrival_slo.csv**](/root/paper/foyer/paper/data/arrival_slo.csv:25) records moves of **8.22** for `foyfix_hc` and **3.22** for `aimd2`. Among the **eleven controller arms** named in the paper, every other absolute move is at most **2.46**. The qualifier matters: four *static* rows in that CSV exceed 2.5.

A literal sweep of printed numbers against CSV data cells still finds **15 occurrences covering ten distinct values**. Most are reconstructible but have no cell for the printed result: **43.7** and **3.5** are SLO differences; **2.44** and **93.3** are controller-count percentages; **13.89** and **13.15** are full-precision wall-time ratios; **6.3** is a static hit-rate difference. **29.1 s** is represented as **29,115.3 ms** in `elasticity_x.csv`; **6.7** is in a `gate_attrib.csv` comment, not a column. The **101,432-token** pool appears in metadata, not `paper/data/*.csv` ([sec_xtrace.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_xtrace.tex:91), [sec_results.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_results.tex:226), [sec_background.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_background.tex:38)). These are traceability gaps, not evidence that the underlying arithmetic is wrong.

## (5) Same number, different meaning

**Mostly MATCH on disambiguation.** The manuscript uses units, signs, run names or explicit denominator notes for the substantive collisions I found: **0.38** (elasticity versus running/live ratio), **0.9** (low-tier positive distance, high-tier negative distance, and wall-time share), **1.24** (projection ratio versus frontier distance), **1.41** (engine prompt ratio versus running count), **5.1** (segment SD versus replicate spread), and **20.6, 22.4, 27.4, 32.4, 36.7, 39.7** across seconds, milliseconds, minutes, percentages and token millions ([sec_results.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_results.tex:511), [sec_model.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_model.tex:268), [sec_discussion.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_discussion.tex:71)). I found no second manuscript meaning for **0.55**: it is the SF-off frontier distance. **1.79** consistently means the base arm’s projection/occupancy ratio.

The specifically named near pairs are separated correctly: **36.3%** is `cap1` idle share and **36.4%** is the device-only realization floor; **80.6%** is `foyer_hc`’s round-1+ hit rate and **80.2%** is `bres`’s ([sec_model.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_model.tex:96), [sec_results.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_results.tex:376)). **92.4%** labels the selected static host-tier endpoint; **92.5%** labels both the rounded device-only best (`cap1`, 92.5464%) and the rounded maximum among the nineteen host-tier runs (`pairA_cap2hc`, 92.4539%). The unqualified “best realizes 92.5%” could name `cap1` explicitly ([sec_model.tex](/root/paper/foyer/paper/sec_model.tex:236)); the more consequential unmarked change of host-tier *set* is the mismatch in (1).

## Acceptance assessment

My rough **current** judgment for a CCF-B / CAS Q2 target is **15–30%**, not a reviewer score or a forecast. The raw-log checks, exposed negative result, and unusually candid limits help. The narrow hardware/workload base, frontier sensitivity to repeats, pending measurements, and the two new internal inconsistencies above leave a skeptical reviewer with material grounds to push back. Resolving the in-flight runs and correcting the two passages would strengthen the case; I have not counted those known in-flight items as defects in this audit.
